=== PROMPT VARIANT ===

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums. A good subsequence is defined as a subsequence of nums where the absolute difference between any two consecutive elements in the subsequence is exactly 1.
Return the sum of all possible good subsequences of nums.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
Note that a subsequence of size 1 is considered good by definition.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,1]
Output: 14
Explanation:

Good subsequences are: [1], [2], [1], [1,2], [2,1], [1,2,1].
The sum of elements in these subsequences is 14.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [3,4,5]
Output: 40
Explanation:

Good subsequences are: [3], [4], [5], [3,4], [4,5], [3,4,5].
The sum of elements in these subsequences is 40.


 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
0 <= nums[i] <= 10^5

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

MOD = 10**9+7
class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        max_val = max(nums)
        cnt = [0]*(max_val+3)
        sm = [0]*(max_val+3)
        ans = 0
        for v in nums:
            left = cnt[v-1] if v > 0 else 0
            right = cnt[v+1]  # v+1 <= max_val+1
            ext_cnt = (left + right) % MOD
            left_sum = sm[v-1] if v > 0 else 0
            right_sum = sm[v+1]
            ext_sum = (left_sum + right_sum + v * ext_cnt) % MOD
            total_new = (ext_sum + v) % MOD
            # update
            cnt[v] = (cnt[v] + ext_cnt + 1) % MOD
            sm[v] = (sm[v] + ext_sum + v) % MOD
            ans = (ans + total_new) % MOD
        return ans

Need ensure v * ext_cnt: v up to 1e5, ext_cnt modulo up to 1e9, product 1e14 fits Python. Fine. Could use % after.

If ext_cnt modulo, okay. But if left+right >= MOD, using modulo in product is okay. For count update, ext_cnt + 1 modulo. Good.

Let's maybe write code elegantly:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        max_val = max(nums)
        cnt = [0] * (max_val + 3)
        total_sum = [0] * (max_val + 3)
        ans = 0
        
        for v in nums:
            prev_cnt = cnt[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0
            next_cnt = cnt[v + 1]
            ext_cnt = (prev_cnt + next_cnt) % MOD
            
            prev_sum = total_sum[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0
            next_sum = total_sum[v + 1]
            ext_sum = (prev_sum + next_sum + v * ext_cnt) % MOD
            
            new_sum = (ext_sum + v) % MOD
            
            cnt[v] = (cnt[v] + ext_cnt + 1) % MOD
            total_sum[v] = (total_sum[v] + new_sum) % MOD
            ans = (ans + new_sum) % MOD
        
        return ans
```

Wait, `total_sum[v] = (total_sum[v] + new_sum) % MOD` where `new_sum = ext_sum + v`. But `new_sum` is sum of all new subsequences ending at current index (extensions + singleton). Yes. Earlier I wrote `sm[v] = sm[v] + ext_sum + v`; same. Good.

But note `ext_sum` already includes sum of extended subsequences, not including singleton. `new_sum` includes singleton. Good.

i3 v1: prev_cnt=cnt0=0, next_cnt=cnt2=2, ext_cnt=2, ext_sum=sum2+1*2=7, new_sum=8, cnt1=1+3=4, sum1=1+8=9, ans14. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block." So final answer should be:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        max_val = max(nums)
        cnt = [0] * (max_val + 3)
        sm = [0] * (max_val + 3)
        ans = 0

        for v in nums:
            prev_cnt = cnt[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0
            next_cnt = cnt[v + 1]
            ext_cnt = (prev_cnt + next_cnt) % MOD

            prev_sum = sm[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0
            next_sum = sm[v + 1]
            ext_sum = (prev_sum + next_sum + v * ext_cnt) % MOD

            added = (ext_sum + v) % MOD

            cnt[v] = (cnt[v] + ext_cnt + 1) % MOD
            sm[v] = (sm[v] + added) % MOD
            ans = (ans + added) % MOD

        return ans
```

Let's double-check variable names: `sm` for sum. Good.

Potential issue: In `ext_sum`, we use `v * ext_cnt`. But `ext_cnt` is modulo count of previous subsequences with last value v-1 or v+1. However, `prev_cnt` and `next_cnt` are modulo counts. Suppose actual count = MOD + 1, ext_cnt = 1. ext_sum = prev_sum + next_sum + v*1. But actual ext_sum = prev_sum + next_sum + v*(MOD+1) = prev_sum+next_sum+v mod. Good. But what about `prev_sum` and `next_sum` are modulo sums of old subsequences. Actual sum of old sums = prev_sum_actual + next_sum_actual. Mod = prev_sum_mod + next_sum_mod. Good. So ext_sum correct.

Potential issue: `cnt[v] = (cnt[v] + ext_cnt + 1) % MOD`. Here `ext_cnt` is modulo count of extensions, `1` singleton. If actual ext_count = MOD+1, ext_cnt=1, actual new count = old + MOD+2, mod = old+2. We add 1+1=2. Good.

Potential issue: `sm[v] = (sm[v] + added) % MOD`. `added` is modulo sum of new subsequences ending at current index. Actual new sum = ext_sum_actual + v. Mod = added. Good.

Potential issue: `ans = (ans + added) % MOD`. Good.

Let's think about if `added` should be `(ext_sum + v) % MOD` or `(ext_sum + v % MOD) % MOD`; v small, same. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "nums = [1,2,3,4,5]" and if our DP's code has no syntax errors. Let's review syntax:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        max_val = max(nums)
        cnt = [0] * (max_val + 3)
        sm = [0] * (max_val + 3)
        ans = 0

        for v in nums:
            prev_cnt = cnt[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0
            next_cnt = cnt[v + 1]
            ext_cnt = (prev_cnt + next_cnt) % MOD

            prev_sum = sm[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0
            next_sum = sm[v + 1]
            ext_sum = (prev_sum + next_sum + v * ext_cnt) % MOD

            added = (ext_sum + v) % MOD

            cnt[v] = (cnt[v] + ext_cnt + 1) % MOD
            sm[v] = (sm[v] + added) % MOD
            ans = (ans + added) % MOD

        return ans
```

Syntax okay. `max(nums)` if nums non-empty. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
": "```python\nfrom typing import List\n\nclass Solution:\n    def sumOfGoodSubsequences(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:\n        MOD = 10**9 + 7\n        max_val = max(nums)\n        cnt = [0] * (max_val + 3)\n        sm = [0] * (max_val + 3)\n        ans = 0\n\n        for v in nums:\n            prev_cnt = cnt[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0\n            next_cnt = cnt[v + 1]\n            ext_cnt = (prev_cnt + next_cnt) % MOD\n\n            prev_sum = sm[v - 1] if v > 0 else 0\n            next_sum = sm[v + 1]\n            ext_sum = (prev_sum + next_sum + v * ext_cnt) % MOD\n\n            added = (ext_sum + v) % MOD\n\n            cnt[v] = (cnt[v] + ext_cnt + 1) % MOD\n            sm[v] = (sm[v] + added) % MOD\n            ans = (ans + added) % MOD\n\n        return ans\n```"
